  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.228, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, yeah, thanks. Well, it's... it's kind of embarrassing, honestly, but I'm here because of elevators. Which sounds stupid, I know. I work in construction, right? I'm a laborer, so I'm on job sites all day, hauling materials, doing demo, whatever the foreman needs. So I'm around heavy stuff all the time. And the thing is, I know elevators aren't really dangerous. I mean, I see how buildings are put together, I know there are cables and brakes and safety inspections and all that. I know the facts. \n\nBut man, when I have to actually get in one, especially those finished, enclosed ones when a project is wrapping up... I just, I get so scared. It's like this heavy feeling that I'm going to be trapped in there and the doors won't open and I'll just be stuck inside this little metal box with no way out. And the most frustrating part is that my brain absolutely knows it's fine, but I just can't make myself feel normal about it. I'll stand there waiting for the doors to open, trying to talk myself down, like, \"Come on, it's just an elevator, you've ridden a million of them,\" but no matter how much I try to logic it out, I can't shake that feeling of being trapped. It just takes over my whole mind, and it's really starting to become a hassle at work because you can't exactly take the stairs every single time when you're working on a ten-story building, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this kind of intense fear or feeling of being trapped in situations other than being in elevators?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just the elevators. I mean, I can be in small spaces, that's not the problem. Like, on the job site, sometimes I have to crawl into these really tight crawlspaces under a house or squeeze between framing to run some wire or check a pipe, and that doesn't bother me at all. I can be in a tiny closet, I can be in a small bathroom, whatever. It's not the size of the space, I don't think. It's... it's just the elevator itself. \n\nAnd it's weird, right? Because you'd think if it was about being trapped, I'd hate being in a basement with only one door, or being stuck in the back of a crowded work van. My buddy drives this old Ford Transit and sometimes there's like five of us crammed in the back with all the tools, and I'm totally fine. We're shoulder to shoulder, no windows, completely boxed in, and I don't bat an eye. We'll be stuck in traffic on the highway for an hour and I'm just drinking my coffee, shooting the breeze. \n\nBut you put me in an elevator, even a big one, even one of those freight elevators that's practically the size of my living room, and it's like a switch flips. It's just... it's the doors closing, and knowing I don't have control over when they open again. So, yeah, to answer your question, no, it doesn't happen anywhere else. I don't panic on airplanes, I don't freak out in tunnels, I don't care about being in a crowded room. It's literally just elevators. It's so specific and so annoying because, like I said, I know it doesn't make sense, but I just can't stop it from happening.\n\nDoctor: Is it the thought of the elevator moving or the fear of something happening while you're in it that scares you, or is it more about the feeling of being confined once the doors close?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's not really about the moving part, I don't think. Like, I don't get motion sickness or anything, and I know the cables are strong. I've literally helped install the hoistways, you know? I've seen the counterweights, I know the mechanics of it. So it's not the physical movement up or down, and it's not even necessarily that I think the elevator is going to plummet down the shaft. I know that basically never happens. \n\nIt's... it's definitely the doors. It's that exact moment. Like, I can be standing in the lobby, totally fine, maybe tapping my foot a little, but fine. But the second I step in and turn around, and I see those metal doors start sliding toward each other... man, it's instant. As soon as those doors start sliding shut, my chest gets tight, like someone just pulled a strap around my ribs. My hands start sweating so bad I have to wipe them on my jeans, and my heart just starts pounding in my ears. \n\nAnd all I can think, the only thought in my head, is just, \"I need out. I need to get out of here right now.\" And it doesn't even matter if I'm only going up, like, two floors. It could be a ten-second ride, but the second those doors meet in the middle and click shut, it's like my brain just screams that I'm trapped and I'm never getting out. I'll just stare at the crack between the doors, willing them to open again, just gripping my toolbelt or whatever I'm holding, trying to breathe, but it's like all the air gets sucked out of the room. It's just this immediate, overwhelming panic that hits me the millisecond I'm closed in, and I just can't shake it until I see the hallway again.\n\nDoctor: Is it the loss of control over being able to exit the elevator when the doors close that scares you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, yeah, exactly. That's exactly it. It's the control thing. Because, like I said, I can be in the back of my buddy's van, right? And I'm boxed in, but I know if I really had to, if there was an emergency, I could just yell at him to pull over, or I could kick the back doors open. I have a way out. I have a say in the matter. \n\nBut an elevator? You step in, those doors close, and you are completely at the mercy of the machine. You press a button and you just have to wait and hope it decides to let you out when it's supposed to. If it stops between floors, what are you gonna do? You can't pry those doors open from the inside, not really, not without a pry bar, and even then, you're just staring at a concrete wall in the shaft. You push the little alarm button, and maybe it rings, maybe someone answers the intercom, but you're still just stuck there, waiting for someone else to come fix it. \n\nAnd that's what gets me. It's that absolute zero control. I'm a hands-on person, you know? In construction, if something's wrong, you grab a tool and you fix it. You cut a hole, you demo a wall, you make it work. But in that metal box, I can't do anything. I'm just trapped. And my brain just loops on that thought. Like, \"You can't get out. You can't get out. You have to wait for the doors.\" And waiting for those doors when you feel like you can't breathe... man, it's the longest ten seconds of your life. So yeah, it's definitely the control. It's knowing I can't just walk away if I want to.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of elevators been present for as long as you can remember, or is it something that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, definitely not my whole life. Not at all. I mean, I've been doing construction for years, right? I used to ride those temporary construction hoists up the side of half-built high-rises, just standing on a metal grate with the wind blowing right through, and it never bothered me one bit. I never even thought about it. So, no, this is... it's actually pretty new. Well, I guess about 11 months now. \n\nYeah, it’s been going on for right around 11 months. And I know exactly when it started, because it was right after we lost our house. Man, that was just... it was a really rough time. We had this string of bad luck, work slowed down for a bit, things piled up, and we just couldn't hold onto the place anymore. We had this nice little single-story house, you just park in the driveway and walk right in the front door, no stairs, no nothing. But we lost it, and we had to scramble to find somewhere we could actually afford on short notice. \n\nWe ended up having to move into this apartment building across town, and of course, the only unit they had available was an upper-floor apartment. Fifth floor. And you know, moving all our stuff, the heavy tool chests and the dressers, up to the fifth floor was a total nightmare, so we were using the building's elevator all day. And it was just... I don't know, something about that move, or having to rely on that specific elevator, or just the stress of losing the house and being forced into this big concrete building. But that's exactly when this whole thing kicked off. Before we moved into that upper-floor place 11 months ago, I wouldn't have even blinked at an elevator doors closing. Now it's just a whole ordeal every single time I come home with my groceries.\n\nDoctor: Was there something specific about the elevator in your apartment building where you first started experiencing this fear that you think might have triggered it?\n\nPatient: Honestly? No. I've racked my brain about this, like, a hundred times, trying to figure out if it jerked weird or if the lights flickered or something, but... no. It's just a completely standard, run-of-the-mill passenger elevator. I think it's an older Otis model, you know, the ones with the brushed steel panels that always have smudges and fingerprints all over them no matter how much the building super wipes them down. And the floor is just that cheap, speckled linoleum tile that we used to rip out of commercial offices all the time back in the day. \n\nIt doesn't even make any sketchy noises. Like, as someone in construction, I know what a failing motor or a bad sheave sounds like, right? You hear grinding or squeaking, or you feel a vibration in the floor deck, and you know something's off with the alignment. But this one? Smooth as butter. It just has that little standard chime when it gets to your floor, and the hum of the ventilation fan. Honestly, the fan is probably the loudest thing in there. \n\nAnd it didn't get stuck on moving day, either, which is what everyone always asks me. We took it up and down, up and down, loading the couch, the mattress, all those heavy tool chests... and it worked perfectly fine. It wasn't jerky, it didn't drop an inch when we loaded the heavy stuff, the electric eye on the door worked perfectly every time we blocked it with a box. So, I mean, if you're asking if there was some big traumatic event with that specific machine, like a cable snapping or the power going out while I was in it, there really wasn't. \n\nIt was just... I don't know, it was just a regular metal box. I keep thinking about the smell of it, too, it always smells like industrial pine cleaner and sometimes wet dog, because Mrs. Higgins on the third floor has this ancient golden retriever that she walks right around the time I get off shift, and... well, anyway, no. Nothing specific about the elevator itself was broken or scary. Mechanically speaking, it's a perfectly fine piece of equipment. It's just my brain that decided to make it a problem, I guess. Because I can picture every inch of that cab, the round plastic buttons, the little digital floor display with the red numbers, and none of it is actually dangerous, but man, I just look at it and my stomach drops.\n\nDoctor: Did something stressful or traumatic happen around the time you started experiencing this fear of elevators, besides losing your house and moving into the apartment?\n\nPatient: No, no, I mean... nothing else like that. Just losing the house and having to pack up and move to that apartment. And honestly, doc, that was more than enough on its own, you know? Like, my whole life was just completely turned upside down just from that one thing, so I didn't really need anything else to go wrong. \n\nBecause besides the move, my routine was pretty much the exact same as it always is. I'd wake up at, like, 5:00 AM, fill up my thermos, and head out to the job site. We were doing this big commercial framing job over off Route 9 at the time—lots of heavy lifting, lots of overtime, just trying to make up the money we lost—and then I'd just come home, eat whatever was easy, and crash. There wasn't really room or time for anything else to happen, traumatic or otherwise, because I was just working myself to the bone and dealing with the fallout of the house. \n\nI mean, nobody died, I didn't get into a car wreck, nobody got sick or anything like that. My crew boss was being a bit of a jerk about my hours for a minute there, just because I had to take a couple afternoons off to sign lease papers and move boxes and deal with the landlord, but that's just construction, you know? Guys get on your case if you aren't pulling your weight that week. It wasn't a big trauma or anything. It was really just the stress of the house. \n\nYou just get so used to a certain way of living, right? I can only do it if somebody I really trust is standing right there in the car with me. Like my buddy Dave on my crew, he's a good guy, I've known him for years. If he's standing right next to me, I can kind of white-knuckle it. It's just... it's the doors closing, and knowing I don't have control over when they open again. So, yeah, to answer your question, no, it doesn't happen anywhere else. I don't panic on airplanes, I don't freak out in tunnels, I don't care about being in a crowded room. It's literally just elevators. It's so specific and so annoying because, like I said, I know it doesn't make sense, but I just can't stop it from happening.\n\nDoctor: Is it the thought of the elevator moving or the fear of something happening while you're in it that scares you, or is it more about the feeling of being confined once the doors close?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's not really about the moving part, I don't think. Like, I don't get motion sickness or anything, and I know the cables are strong. I've literally helped install the hoistways, you know? I've seen the counterweights, I know the mechanics of it. So it's not the physical movement up or down, and it's not even necessarily that I think the elevator is going to plummet down the shaft. I know that basically never happens. \n\nIt's... it's definitely the doors. It's that exact moment. Like, I can be standing in the lobby, totally fine, maybe tapping my foot a little, but fine. But the second I step in and turn around, and I see those metal doors start sliding toward each other... man, it's instant. As soon as those doors start sliding shut, my chest gets tight, like someone just pulled a strap around my ribs. My hands start sweating so bad I have to wipe them on my jeans, and my heart just starts pounding in my ears. \n\nAnd all I can think, the only thought in my head, is just, \"I need out. I need to get out of here right now.\" And it doesn't even matter if I'm only going up, like, two floors. It could be a ten-second ride, but the second those doors meet in the middle and click shut, it's like my brain just screams that I'm trapped and I'm never getting out. I'll just stare at the crack between the doors, willing them to open again, just gripping my toolbelt or whatever I'm holding, trying to breathe, but it's like all the air gets sucked out of the room. It's just this immediate, overwhelming panic that hits me the millisecond I'm closed in, and I just can't shake it until I see the hallway again.\n\nDoctor: Is it the loss of control over being able to exit the elevator when the doors close that scares you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, yeah, exactly. That's exactly it. It's the control thing. Because, like I said, I can be in the back of my buddy's van, right? And I'm boxed in, but I know if I really had to, if there was an emergency, I could just yell at him to pull over, or I could kick the back doors open. I have a way out. I have a say in the matter. \n\nBut an elevator? You step in, those doors close, and you are completely at the mercy of the machine. You press a button and you just have to wait and hope it decides to let you out when it's supposed to. If it stops between floors, what are you gonna do? You can't pry those doors open from the inside, not really, not without a pry bar, and even then, you're just staring at a concrete wall in the shaft. You push the little alarm button, and maybe it rings, maybe someone answers the intercom, but you're still just stuck there, waiting for someone else to come fix it. \n\nAnd that's what gets me. It's that absolute zero control. I'm a hands-on person, you know? In construction, if something's wrong, you grab a tool and you fix it. You cut a hole, you demo a wall, you make it work. But in that metal box, I can't do anything. I'm just trapped. And my brain just loops on that thought. Like, \"You can't get out. You can't get out. You have to wait for the doors.\" And waiting for those doors when you feel like you can't breathe... man, it's the longest ten seconds of your life. So yeah, it's definitely the control. It's knowing I can't just walk away if I want to.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of elevators been present for as long as you can remember, or is it something that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, definitely not my whole life. Not at all. I mean, I've been doing construction for years, right? I used to ride those temporary construction hoists up the side of half-built high-rises, just standing on a metal grate with the wind blowing right through, and it never bothered me one bit. I never even thought about it. So, no, this is... it's actually pretty new. Well, I guess about 11 months now. \n\nYeah, it’s been going on for right around 11 months. And I know exactly when it started, because it was right after we lost our house. Man, that was just... it was a really rough time. We had this string of bad luck, work slowed down for a bit, things piled up, and we just couldn't hold onto the place anymore. We had this nice little single-story house, you just park in the driveway and walk right in the front door, no stairs, no nothing. But we lost it, and we had to scramble to find somewhere we could actually afford on short notice. \n\nWe ended up having to move into this apartment building across town, and of course, the only unit they had available was an upper-floor apartment. Fifth floor. And you know, moving all our stuff, the heavy tool chests and the dressers, up to the fifth floor was a total nightmare, so we were using the building's elevator all day. And it was just... I don't know, something about that move, or having to rely on that specific elevator, or just the stress of losing the house and being forced into this big concrete building. But that's exactly when this whole thing kicked off. Before we moved into that upper-floor place 11 months ago, I wouldn't have even blinked at an elevator doors closing. Now it's just a whole ordeal every single time I come home with my groceries.\n\nDoctor: Was there something specific about the elevator in your apartment building where you first started experiencing this fear that you think might have triggered it?\n\nPatient: Honestly? No. I've racked my brain about this, like, a hundred times, trying to figure out if it jerked weird or if the lights flickered or something, but... no. It's just a completely standard, run-of-the-mill passenger elevator. I think it's an older Otis model, you know, the ones with the brushed steel panels that always have smudges and fingerprints all over them no matter how much the building super wipes them down. And the floor is just that cheap, speckled linoleum tile that we used to rip out of commercial offices all the time back in the day. \n\nIt doesn't even make any sketchy noises. Like, as someone in construction, I know what a failing motor or a bad sheave sounds like, right? You hear grinding or squeaking, or you feel a vibration in the floor deck, and you know something's off with the alignment. But this one? Smooth as butter. It just has that little standard chime when it gets to your floor, and the hum of the ventilation fan. Honestly, the fan is probably the loudest thing in there. \n\nAnd it didn't get stuck on moving day, either, which is what everyone always asks me. We took it up and down, up and down, loading the couch, the mattress, all those heavy tool chests... and it worked perfectly fine. It wasn't jerky, it didn't drop an inch when we loaded the heavy stuff, the electric eye on the door worked perfectly every time we blocked it with a box. So, I mean, if you're asking if there was some big traumatic event with that specific machine, like a cable snapping or the power going out while I was in it, there really wasn't. \n\nIt was just... I don't know, it was just a regular metal box. I keep thinking about the smell of it, too, it always smells like industrial pine cleaner and sometimes wet dog, because Mrs. Higgins on the third floor has this ancient golden retriever that she walks right around the time I get off shift, and... well, anyway, no. Nothing specific about the elevator itself was broken or scary. Mechanically speaking, it's a perfectly fine piece of equipment. It's just my brain that decided to make it a problem, I guess. Because I can picture every inch of that cab, the round plastic buttons, the little digital floor display with the red numbers, and none of it is actually dangerous, but man, I just look at it and my stomach drops.\n\nDoctor: Did something stressful or traumatic happen around the time you started experiencing this fear of elevators, besides losing your house and moving into the apartment?\n\nPatient: No, no, I mean... nothing else like that. Just losing the house and having to pack up and move to that apartment. And honestly, doc, that was more than enough on its own, you know? Like, my whole life was just completely turned upside down just from that one thing, so I didn't really need anything else to go wrong. \n\nBecause besides the move, my routine was pretty much the exact same as it always is. I'd wake up at, like, 5:00 AM, fill up my thermos, and head out to the job site. We were doing this big commercial framing job over off Route 9 at the time—lots of heavy lifting, lots of overtime, just trying to make up the money we lost—and then I'd just come home, eat whatever was easy, and crash. There wasn't really room or time for anything else to happen, traumatic or otherwise, because I was just working myself to the bone and dealing with the fallout of the house. \n\nI mean, nobody died, I didn't get into a car wreck, nobody got sick or anything like that. My crew boss was being a bit of a jerk about my hours for a minute there, just because I had to take a couple afternoons off to sign lease papers and move boxes and deal with the landlord, but that's just construction, you know? Guys get on your case if you aren't pulling your weight that week. It wasn't a big trauma or anything. It was really just the stress of the house. \n\nYou just get so used to a certain way of living, right? One day you have your own driveway and your own front door, you can just walk right into your kitchen, and the next you're lugging your muddy steel-toes into a concrete lobby, smelling that weird pine cleaner, and waiting for a metal box to take you up to the fifth floor where you can hear your neighbors through the walls. It was a huge adjustment. So, yeah, no other big crazy events or accidents or anything. Just that one massive, awful thing with the house.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this fear of elevators in other buildings or locations, or is it primarily when you're in your apartment building's elevator?\n\nPatient: Oh, it's definitely not just the apartment anymore. I really wish it was just that one, but it’s kind of spilled over into everything else. Like, any elevator, anywhere. So, mostly, I just avoid them entirely. Most days I just take the stairs, you know? At the apartment, I’m hiking up five flights of stairs every single day. And let me tell you, after a ten- or twelve-hour shift on your feet, hauling lumber or framing out walls, the absolute last thing your knees want to do is climb five flights of concrete stairs, especially if I'm lugging bags of groceries or a gallon of milk. My calves are absolutely screaming by the time I get to my front door. But I still do it, because looking at those sliding metal doors in the lobby just makes my stomach tie itself in knots. \n\nAnd it’s the exact same thing at work on the job sites, too. We have those temporary lifts, the hoists I was telling you about earlier, or sometimes we're finishing up a commercial building where the actual indoor elevators are already turned on and operational. And I just... I take the stairs there, too. Even if the work is up on the eighth floor and I'm carrying my heavy tool belt and my thermos and everything. I just find the stairwell and start climbing. The guys on my crew look at me like I'm crazy, sweating through my shirt before the shift even really starts, but I just brush it off and tell them I'm getting my cardio in or keeping my legs strong. \n\nBut sometimes... you know, sometimes you just can't avoid it. Like if an elevator or a construction lift is literally the only way up to where the work is, because the stairs aren't built yet or they're blocked off for pouring concrete. When that happens, I usually try to pull my crew boss aside and just ask for different work. I'll be like, \"Hey, let me handle the ground floor staging today,\" or \"I'll sort the materials down here in the loading bay.\" I try to play it off like I'm just being helpful where they need it. \n\nBut if I absolutely, positively have to ride the thing to keep my job... I can only do it if somebody I really trust is standing right there in the car with me. Like my buddy Dave on my crew, he's a good guy, I've known him for years. If he's standing right next to me, I can kind of white-knuckle it. I make him stand close and I just stare at the sticker on his hard hat and talk his ear off about baseball or truck parts or whatever, just to keep my mind completely distracted from the fact that we're trapped in this box. If my husband is with me at the apartment, it's the same thing, I hold onto his arm and just close my eyes. But yeah, if I'm by myself? No way. I'm taking the stairs or I'm not going up. It's just too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can never fully prepare yourself for the loss of control when you step into an elevator, no matter how many times you've ridden one before?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. You'd think I'd be able to get a handle on it by now, right? Like, I stand out there in the hallway or the lobby, and I do this whole mental checklist thing. I tell myself, \"Okay, it's just a machine, it's got safety brakes, you've ridden these a million times before the move, it's gonna take exactly thirty seconds.\" I try to pump myself up like I'm about to go do some crazy heavy lift on the job site. I take deep breaths, I wipe my boots on the mat, I stare at the little light above the door just waiting for the ding. And in my head, I feel totally prepared. I'm telling myself, \"You got this, it's no big deal, just step in and stand there.\" \n\nBut then I actually step inside. And it's like all that prep work just completely flies right out the window. It doesn't matter how much I tried to logic my way through it beforehand. Because as soon as those doors start sliding shut, my chest gets tight. Like, really tight, almost like somebody strapped a heavy leather tool belt around my ribs and pulled it tight as they could. My hands start sweating so bad I have to wipe them on my jeans, and my brain just instantly goes into this sort of overdrive. All I can think is, \"I need out. I need out right now.\" \n\nAnd the crazy part is, it doesn't even matter how short the trip is. Even if it’s only going up a few floors, you know? Like, a ride that's gonna be practically over before it even really starts, my brain just doesn't care. It just screams at me that I'm trapped in there. Because once those metal doors click together, that's it, you know? You're not in control anymore. You can't just push them back open with your hands if you change your mind. You're totally at the mercy of the cables and the buttons and the motor. And I just stand there, sweating, staring at the crack between the doors, just praying for them to open up again so I can breathe. So yeah, you really can't prepare for it. You can tell yourself whatever you want while you're standing safe in the hallway, but the absolute second that metal box seals you in, it's just... boom. You lose all control. It’s wild, honestly, because I’m a practical person, I know how things are built, but my body just completely takes over.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of elevators affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Well, with my family and friends... I mean, my husband gets a little annoyed sometimes, you know, when we go somewhere and I make him hike up the stairs with me, or my buddy Dave on the crew who has to stand there while I grip his arm if I absolutely have to ride one. They think it's a little weird, sure, but it hasn't, like, ruined my marriage or made my friends stop talking to me or anything like that. Like, I'm still pushing through. I'm still getting to work every single morning, and I'm still going to my GED classes a couple nights a week. Because I'm taking the stairs absolutely everywhere I go, I end up running late half the time. Taking all those extra flights of stairs just leaves me completely worn out before I even pick up a hammer. And like I mentioned earlier about trying to swap tasks with the other guys? Turning down those elevator-only jobs, or making up excuses so I don't have to ride the hoist up to the top floors... it's making me a lot less useful on some of our construction assignments. The foreman needs people who can just go where they're told, when they're told, no questions asked. If I'm always saying, \"Oh, hey, let me stay down here and sort the lumber instead,\" I'm not really pulling my full weight where they actually need me. I'm just... I'm getting by, but I'm so physically worn out and always running behind, and I just feel like I'm letting my crew down because I can't just be normal and ride the stupid lift like everybody else.\n\nDoctor: Have you considered looking for a different job because of your fear of elevators?\n\nPatient: Honestly? No. I mean, I haven't actually looked for anything else, or even really seriously thought about quitting. I know I was just complaining about how exhausting it is, and believe me, when I'm staring up at an eight-story stairwell with fifty pounds of gear on my back, a tiny part of my brain is definitely screaming at me to just walk away. I just can't imagine throwing in the towel and starting completely over just because of this. Like I said, I'm still getting to work every day, and I'm still showing up for my GED classes, so I haven't let it completely stop me from living my life or anything like that. I'm pushing through. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I wouldn't say it's affecting my overall happiness or fulfillment. I mean, like I said, I'm generally a pretty happy person. I love my husband, I'm proud of the work I do, and I'm really excited about getting my GED. I'm actually looking forward to the future, you know? So, no, I don't feel unfulfilled or unhappy in the grand scheme of things. It's just... this one thing. It's like having a pebble in your shoe, you know? You're still walking, you're still getting where you need to go, but every step is just a little bit annoying and painful. That's what this elevator thing is like. It's a massive, exhausting, sweaty pebble in my shoe. 

But it doesn't ruin the whole walk. Like, when I'm on the job site and I'm actually doing the framing, or when I'm pouring concrete, I feel great. I feel strong and capable and like I'm contributing to something real. And when I'm at home, sitting on the couch with my husband, watching some ridiculous reality show and eating popcorn, I'm perfectly content. I'm not sitting there thinking, "Oh, my life is so empty and meaningless because I can't ride an elevator." 

It's just that the *process* of getting to those good moments is so much harder than it needs to be. It's the logistics of it all that drain me. The planning, the excuses, the physical toll of the stairs... it's just a constant drain on my energy. But it doesn't touch my actual happiness, if that makes sense. It just makes me really, really tired. And frustrated. Definitely frustrated. But not unfulfilled. I know what I want out of life, and I'm going after it, even if I have to take the stairs to get there.

I mean, I can be in small spaces, that's not the problem. We're shoulder to shoulder, no windows, completely boxed in, and I don't bat an eye. It's just... it's the doors closing, and knowing I don't have control over when they open again. So, yeah, to answer your question, no, it doesn't happen anywhere else. I don't panic on airplanes, I don't freak out in tunnels, I don't care about being in a crowded room. It's literally just elevators. It's so specific and so annoying because, like I said, I know it doesn't make sense, but I just can't stop it from happening.\n\nDoctor: Is it the thought of the elevator moving or the fear of something happening while you're in it that scares you, or is it more about the feeling of being confined once the doors close?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's not really about the moving part, I don't think. Like, I don't get motion sickness or anything, and I know the cables are strong. I've literally helped install the hoistways, you know? I've seen the counterweights, I know the mechanics of it. So it's not the physical movement up or down, and it's not even necessarily that I think the elevator is going to plummet down the shaft. I know that basically never happens. \n\nIt's... it's definitely the doors. It's that exact moment. Like, I can be standing in the lobby, totally fine, maybe tapping my foot a little, but fine. As soon as those doors start sliding shut, my chest gets tight, like someone just pulled a strap around my ribs. I'll just stare at the crack between the doors, willing them to open again, just gripping my toolbelt or whatever I'm holding, trying to breathe, but it's like all the air gets sucked out of the room. It's just this immediate, overwhelming panic that hits me the millisecond I'm closed in, and I just can't shake it until I see the hallway again.\n\nDoctor: Is it the loss of control over being able to exit the elevator when the doors close that scares you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, yeah, exactly. That's exactly it. It's the control thing. Because, like I said, I can be in the back of my buddy's van, right? And I'm boxed in, but I know if I really had to, if there was an emergency, I could just yell at him to pull over, or I could kick the back doors open. I have a way out. I have a say in the matter. \n\nBut an elevator? You step in, those doors close, and you are completely at the mercy of the machine. You press a button and you just have to wait and hope it decides to let you out when it's supposed to. If it stops between floors, what are you gonna do? You can't pry those doors open from the inside, not really, not without a pry bar, and even then, you're just staring at a concrete wall in the shaft. You push the little alarm button, and maybe it rings, maybe someone answers the intercom, but you're still just stuck there, waiting for someone else to come fix it. \n\nAnd that's what gets me. It's that absolute zero control. I'm a hands-on person, you know? In construction, if something's wrong, you grab a tool and you fix it. You cut a hole, you demo a wall, you make it work. But in that metal box, I can't do anything. I'm just trapped. And my brain just loops on that thought. Like, \"You can't get out. You can't get out. You have to wait for the doors.\" And waiting for those doors when you feel like you can't breathe... man, it's the longest ten seconds of your life. So yeah, it's definitely the control. It's knowing I can't just walk away if I want to.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of elevators been present for as long as you can remember, or is it something that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, definitely not my whole life. Not at all. I mean, I've been doing construction for years, right? I used to ride those temporary construction hoists up the side of half-built high-rises, just standing on a metal grate with the wind blowing right through, and it never bothered me one bit. I never even thought about it. So, no, this is... it's actually pretty new. Well, I guess about 11 months now. \n\nYeah, it’s been going on for right around 11 months. And I know exactly when it started, because it was right after we lost our house. Man, that was just... it was a really rough time. We had this string of bad luck, work slowed down for a bit, things piled up, and we just couldn't hold onto the place anymore. We had this nice little single-story house, you just park in the driveway and walk right in the front door, no stairs, no nothing. But that's exactly when this whole thing kicked off. Before we moved into that upper-floor place 11 months ago, I wouldn't have even blinked at an elevator doors closing. Now it's just a whole ordeal every single time I come home with my groceries.\n\nDoctor: Was there something specific about the elevator in your apartment building where you first started experiencing this fear that you think might have triggered it?\n\nPatient: Honestly? I think it's an older Otis model, you know, the ones with the brushed steel panels that always have smudges and fingerprints all over them no matter how much the building super wipes them down. You hear grinding or squeaking, or you feel a vibration in the floor deck, and you know something's off with the alignment. But this one? Smooth as butter. Just that one massive, awful thing with the house.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this fear of elevators in other buildings or locations, or is it primarily when you're in your apartment building's elevator?\n\nPatient: Oh, it's definitely not just the apartment anymore. I make him stand close and I just stare at the sticker on his hard hat and talk his ear off about baseball or truck parts or whatever, just to keep my mind completely distracted from the fact that we're trapped in this box. If my husband is with me at the apartment, it's the same thing, I hold onto his arm and just close my eyes. But yeah, if I'm by myself? No way. I'm taking the stairs or I'm not going up. It's just too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can never fully prepare yourself for the loss of control when you step into an elevator, no matter how many times you've ridden one before?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. You'd think I'd be able to get a handle on it by now, right? Like, I stand out there in the hallway or the lobby, and I do this whole mental checklist thing. I tell myself, \"Okay, it's just a machine, it's got safety brakes, you've ridden these a million times before the move, it's gonna take exactly thirty seconds.\" I try to pump myself up like I'm about to go do some crazy heavy lift on the job site. I take deep breaths, I wipe my boots on the mat, I stare at the little light above the door just waiting for the ding. And in my head, I feel totally prepared. I'm telling myself, \"You got this, it's no big deal, just step in and stand there.\" \n\nBut then I actually step inside. And it's like all that prep work just completely flies right out the window. It doesn't matter how much I tried to logic my way through it beforehand. Because as soon as those doors start sliding shut, my chest gets tight. Like, really tight, almost like somebody strapped a heavy leather tool belt around my ribs and pulled it tight as they could. My hands start sweating so bad I have to wipe them on my jeans, and my brain just instantly goes into this sort of overdrive. All I can think is, \"I need out. I need out right now.\" \n\nAnd the crazy part is, it doesn't even matter how short the trip is. Even if it’s only going up a few floors, you know? Like, a ride that's gonna be practically over before it even really starts, my brain just doesn't care. It just screams at me that I'm trapped in there. Because once those metal doors click together, that's it, you know? You're not in control anymore. You can't just push them back open with your hands if you change your mind. You're totally at the mercy of the cables and the buttons and the motor. And I just stand there, sweating, staring at the crack between the doors, just praying for them to open up again so I can breathe. So yeah, you really can't prepare for it. You can tell yourself whatever you want while you're standing safe in the hallway, but the absolute second that metal box seals you in, it's just... boom. You lose all control. It’s wild, honestly, because I’m a practical person, I know how things are built, but my body just completely takes over.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of elevators affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Well, with my family and friends... I mean, my husband gets a little annoyed sometimes, you know, when we go somewhere and I make him hike up the stairs with me, or my buddy Dave on the crew who has to stand there while I grip his arm if I absolutely have to ride one. They think it's a little weird, sure, but it hasn't, like, ruined my marriage or made my friends stop talking to me or anything like that. They mostly just put up with it and complain about their knees. \n\nBut honestly, doc, where it's really messing things up is just... my everyday life, my actual routine. Like, I'm still pushing through. I'm still getting to work every single morning, and I'm still going to my GED classes a couple nights a week. I dropped out back in the day, so I'm finally trying to get my diploma now, which is a whole big stressful thing on its own, sitting in those little plastic chairs trying to remember math after a ten-hour shift. But I'm going! I'm doing it. \n\nIt's just... this whole elevator thing is making it all so incredibly exhausting. Because I'm taking the stairs absolutely everywhere I go, I end up running late half the time. I'll get to the community center for my GED class, and of course, the room is up on the fourth floor, so I'm hauling myself up the stairwell, and I end up walking into the room ten minutes late, completely out of breath, sweating, and everyone turns and looks at me while I'm trying to quietly find a seat. \n\nAnd at work, it's even worse. Taking all those extra flights of stairs just leaves me completely worn out before I even pick up a hammer. And like I mentioned earlier about trying to swap tasks with the other guys? Turning down those elevator-only jobs, or making up excuses so I don't have to ride the hoist up to the top floors... it's making me a lot less useful on some of our construction assignments. The foreman needs people who can just go where they're told, when they're told, no questions asked. If I'm always saying, \"Oh, hey, let me stay down here and sort the lumber instead,\" I'm not really pulling my full weight where they actually need me. I'm just... I'm getting by, but I'm so physically worn out and always running behind, and I just feel like I'm letting my crew down because I can't just be normal and ride the stupid lift like everybody else.\n\nDoctor: Have you considered looking for a different job because of your fear of elevators?\n\nPatient: Honestly? No. I mean, I haven't actually looked for anything else, or even really seriously thought about quitting. I know I was just complaining about how exhausting it is, and believe me, when I'm staring up at an eight-story stairwell with fifty pounds of gear on my back, a tiny part of my brain is definitely screaming at me to just walk away. But actually leaving? No way. \n\nI've been in construction basically since I dropped out of high school. It’s pretty much all I know how to do. There's something really satisfying about that, you know? Being able to point at a structure and say, \"I built that.\" Plus, the pay is actually pretty decent once you get some years under your belt, and I'm in the union now, so the health insurance and the benefits are really good. Especially without my GED—which, like I said, I'm working on, but I don't have it yet. I'm a laborer. That's just who I am. \n\nSo, yeah, I just... I just deal with it. I figure out workarounds. It’s a massive pain in the butt, and it makes my days way harder than they need to be, but you don't just throw away a solid career because you're terrified of a stupid metal box. You just put your head down and you climb the stairs. Even if it means my knees are shot by the time I'm forty and I'm sweating through my shirt before the morning meeting even starts. I just can't imagine throwing in the towel and starting completely over just because of this. I just have to keep pushing through it, you know?\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your daily life, besides work and relationships, that are being affected by your fear of elevators?\n\nPatient: I mean, honestly... not really, but only because work and my GED classes pretty much *are* my entire daily life right now. Guys get on your case if you aren't pulling your weight that week. It wasn't a big trauma or anything. It was really just the stress of the house. \n\nYou just get so used to a certain way of living, right? One day you have your own driveway and your own front door, you can just walk right into your kitchen, and the next you're lugging your muddy steel-toes into a concrete lobby, smelling that weird pine cleaner, and waiting for a metal box to take you up to the fifth floor where you can hear your neighbors through the walls. It was a huge adjustment. So, yeah, no other big crazy events or accidents or anything. Just that one massive, awful thing with the house.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this fear of elevators in other buildings or locations, or is it primarily when you're in your apartment building's elevator?\n\nPatient: Oh, it's definitely not just the apartment anymore. I really wish it was just that one, but it’s kind of spilled over into everything else. Like, any elevator, anywhere. So, mostly, I just avoid them entirely. Most days I just take the stairs, you know? At the apartment, I’m hiking up five flights of stairs every single day. And let me tell you, after a ten- or twelve-hour shift on your feet, hauling lumber or framing out walls, the absolute last thing your knees want to do is climb five flights of concrete stairs, especially if I'm lugging bags of groceries or a gallon of milk. My calves are absolutely screaming by the time I get to my front door. But I still do it, because looking at those sliding metal doors in the lobby just makes my stomach tie itself in knots. \n\nAnd it’s the exact same thing at work on the job sites, too. We have those temporary lifts, the hoists I was telling you about earlier, or sometimes we're finishing up a commercial building where the actual indoor elevators are already turned on and operational. And I just... I take the stairs there, too. Even if the work is up on the eighth floor and I'm carrying my heavy tool belt and my thermos and everything. I just find the stairwell and start climbing. The guys on my crew look at me like I'm crazy, sweating through my shirt before the shift even really starts, but I just brush it off and tell them I'm getting my cardio in or keeping my legs strong. \n\nBut sometimes... you know, sometimes you just can't avoid it. Like if an elevator or a construction lift is literally the only way up to where the work is, because the stairs aren't built yet or they're blocked off for pouring concrete. When that happens, I usually try to pull my crew boss aside and just ask for different work. I'll be like, \"Hey, let me handle the ground floor staging today,\" or \"I'll sort the materials down here in the loading bay.\" I try to play it off like I'm just being helpful where they need it. \n\nBut if I absolutely, positively have to ride the thing to keep my job... I can only do it if somebody I really trust is standing right there in the car with me. Like my buddy Dave on my crew, he's a good guy, I've known him for years. If he's standing right next to me, I can kind of white-knuckle it. I make him stand close and I just stare at the sticker on his hard hat and talk his ear off about baseball or truck parts or whatever, just to keep my mind completely distracted from the fact that we're trapped in this box. If my husband is with me at the apartment, it's the same thing, I hold onto his arm and just close my eyes. But yeah, if I'm by myself? No way. I'm taking the stairs or I'm not going up. It's just too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can never fully prepare yourself for the loss of control when you step into an elevator, no matter how many times you've ridden one before?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. You'd think I'd be able to get a handle on it by now, right? Like, I stand out there in the hallway or the lobby, and I do this whole mental checklist thing. I tell myself, \"Okay, it's just a machine, it's got safety brakes, you've ridden these a million times before the move, it's gonna take exactly thirty seconds.\" I try to pump myself up like I'm about to go do some crazy heavy lift on the job site. I take deep breaths, I wipe my boots on the mat, I stare at the little light above the door just waiting for the ding. And in my head, I feel totally prepared. I'm telling myself, \"You got this, it's no big deal, just step in and stand there.\" \n\nBut then I actually step inside. And it's like all that prep work just completely flies right out the window. It doesn't matter how much I tried to logic my way through it beforehand. Because as soon as those doors start sliding shut, my chest gets tight. Like, really tight, almost like somebody strapped a heavy leather tool belt around my ribs and pulled it tight as they could. My hands start sweating so bad I have to wipe them on my jeans, and my brain just instantly goes into this sort of overdrive. All I can think is, \"I need out. I need out right now.\" \n\nAnd the crazy part is, it doesn't even matter how short the trip is. Even if it’s only going up a few floors, you know? Like, a ride that's gonna be practically over before it even really starts, my brain just doesn't care. It just screams at me that I'm trapped in there. Because once those metal doors click together, that's it, you know? You're not in control anymore. You can't just push them back open with your hands if you change your mind. You're totally at the mercy of the cables and the buttons and the motor. And I just stand there, sweating, staring at the crack between the doors, just praying for them to open up again so I can breathe. So yeah, you really can't prepare for it. You can tell yourself whatever you want while you're standing safe in the hallway, but the absolute second that metal box seals you in, it's just... boom. You lose all control. It’s wild, honestly, because I’m a practical person, I know how things are built, but my body just completely takes over.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of elevators affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Well, with my family and friends... I mean, my husband gets a little annoyed sometimes, you know, when we go somewhere and I make him hike up the stairs with me, or my buddy Dave on the crew who has to stand there while I grip his arm if I absolutely have to ride one. They think it's a little weird, sure, but it hasn't, like, ruined my marriage or made my friends stop talking to me or anything like that. They mostly just put up with it and complain about their knees. \n\nBut honestly, doc, where it's really messing things up is just... my everyday life, my actual routine. Like, I'm still pushing through. I'm still getting to work every single morning, and I'm still going to my GED classes a couple nights a week. I dropped out back in the day, so I'm finally trying to get my diploma now, which is a whole big stressful thing on its own, sitting in those little plastic chairs trying to remember math after a ten-hour shift. But I'm going! I'm doing it. \n\nIt's just... this whole elevator thing is making it all so incredibly exhausting. Because I'm taking the stairs absolutely everywhere I go, I end up running late half the time. I'll get to the community center for my GED class, and of course, the room is up on the fourth floor, so I'm hauling myself up the stairwell, and I end up walking into the room ten minutes late, completely out of breath, sweating, and everyone turns and looks at me while I'm trying to quietly find a seat. \n\nAnd at work, it's even worse. Taking all those extra flights of stairs just leaves me completely worn out before I even pick up a hammer. And like I mentioned earlier about trying to swap tasks with the other guys? Turning down those elevator-only jobs, or making up excuses so I don't have to ride the hoist up to the top floors... it's making me a lot less useful on some of our construction assignments. The foreman needs people who can just go where they're told, when they're told, no questions asked. If I'm always saying, \"Oh, hey, let me stay down here and sort the lumber instead,\" I'm not really pulling my full weight where they actually need me. I'm just... I'm getting by, but I'm so physically worn out and always running behind, and I just feel like I'm letting my crew down because I can't just be normal and ride the stupid lift like everybody else.\n\nDoctor: Have you considered looking for a different job because of your fear of elevators?\n\nPatient: Honestly? No. I mean, I haven't actually looked for anything else, or even really seriously thought about quitting. I know I was just complaining about how exhausting it is, and believe me, when I'm staring up at an eight-story stairwell with fifty pounds of gear on my back, a tiny part of my brain is definitely screaming at me to just walk away. But actually leaving? No way. \n\nI've been in construction basically since I dropped out of high school. It’s pretty much all I know how to do. And the thing is, I actually really like the work itself. I like working with my hands, I like being outside, I like seeing a site go from just a patch of dirt to a fully framed-out building. There's something really satisfying about that, you know? Being able to point at a structure and say, \"I built that.\" Plus, the pay is actually pretty decent once you get some years under your belt, and I'm in the union now, so the health insurance and the benefits are really good. My husband and I rely on that, especially with how expensive groceries and rent and everything else have gotten lately. \n\nIf I quit, what else would I even do? Especially without my GED—which, like I said, I'm working on, but I don't have it yet. I'd probably have to take a massive pay cut to go work retail or stock shelves on a ground floor somewhere, and I think I'd just go absolutely crazy being cooped up inside all day dealing with customers. I'm a laborer. That's just who I am. \n\nSo, yeah, I just... I just deal with it. I figure out workarounds. It’s a massive pain in the butt, and it makes my days way harder than they need to be, but you don't just throw away a solid career because you're terrified of a stupid metal box. You just put your head down and you climb the stairs. Even if it means my knees are shot by the time I'm forty and I'm sweating through my shirt before the morning meeting even starts. I just can't imagine throwing in the towel and starting completely over just because of this. I just have to keep pushing through it, you know?\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your daily life, besides work and relationships, that are being affected by your fear of elevators?\n\nPatient: I mean, honestly... not really, but only because work and my GED classes pretty much *are* my entire daily life right now. I know I kind of rambled about this a minute ago, but that's really where it hits me the absolute hardest. Like I said, I'm still getting to work every day, and I'm still showing up for my GED classes, so I haven't let it completely stop me from living my life or anything like that. I'm pushing through. But it's just... it's the massive toll it takes on me while I'm trying to do those normal, everyday things, you know? \n\nBecause I'm taking the stairs for absolutely everything, I'm constantly running late. And it's not just a casual 'whoops, I missed the first two minutes' kind of late. It's that awful, sweating, out-of-breath kind of late where you walk into the community center for class and everyone turns around to stare at you. And I'm thirty-four, you know? I'm already older than most of the people in that room, so I already feel a little out of place, and then I have to sit there trying to focus on algebra or reading comprehension while my heart is just pounding out of my chest and my legs are burning just from the climb up to the classroom. \n\nAnd then at work... well, like I was telling you before about swapping tasks, turning down those elevator-only jobs is really starting to make me feel less useful on some of our assignments. Like, the guys will be loading up the temporary hoist to take a bunch of heavy materials up to the upper floors, and I'll have to scramble to find some reason to stay on the ground level. I'll say I need to clean up the staging area, or sort the hardware, or whatever, but the truth is, I'm just avoiding the lift. And taking the stairs when I *do* have to go up just leaves me so completely worn out before the real work even begins. You try carrying a heavy tool belt up six flights of temporary wooden stairs at seven in the morning! \n\nSo, yeah, I guess it's really just those two main things—the job site and trying to get my diploma—but because that's my whole entire schedule from sunup to sundown, it just feels like I'm constantly exhausted and running behind on everything. It just drains the energy right out of me.\n\nDoctor: Are there other activities or hobbies you enjoy that are being affected by your fear of elevators?\n\nPatient: Hobbies? Oh, gosh, no. I mean, I wish I had time or energy for hobbies, but honestly, like I was just saying a minute ago, my plate is completely full just managing the bare minimum right now. Work and the GED classes... that's really it. That's my whole entire week. \n\nAnd honestly, doctor, this whole elevator situation is a huge part of exactly *why* I don't do anything else for fun. I know I keep bringing it back to the same things, but it’s just the truth of what my life looks like right now. I mean, I’m still getting to work every day, and I’m still making it to my classes—I'm not letting it completely stop me from doing what I absolutely *have* to do. I'm showing up. But because I'm forcing myself to take the stairs every single time, I'm just constantly running late, and I am just so physically worn out by the time I get home. \n\nMy husband actually asked me just the other weekend if I wanted to go to the state park for a hike, and I just looked at him and laughed. I was like, \"Babe, I've been hauling myself up and down six flights of concrete stairs all week, my knees are practically turning to dust.\" So, yeah, no hiking for me. \n\nAnd then at the job site, it's just exactly like I mentioned before... having to turn down those elevator-only jobs and making up excuses so I don't have to ride the temporary hoist, it really does make me less useful on some of our construction assignments. I'm spending all this extra physical and mental energy just trying to work around it, taking the long way up, carrying my gear up the stairwells... so when the weekend finally hits, or when I have a free evening, I don't want to do any activities. I don't want to go out. I just want to sit on the couch, ice my legs, and try to recover before I have to do it all over again on Monday. So, yeah, no real hobbies right now. Just trying to survive the week, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing any feelings of anxiety, depression, or hopelessness outside of the situations involving elevators?\n\nPatient: Oh, no. No, definitely not. I mean, I know I've been sitting here complaining for the last twenty minutes about how exhausted I am and how much my knees ache, but that's really just physical, you know? Mentally and emotionally... I'm actually doing okay. I don't feel hopeless or depressed or anything like that. \n\nI mean, sure, I get stressed out sometimes—but who doesn't? Just normal stuff. Like if the bills are piling up and my husband's hours get cut at his job, or when I have a huge math test coming up for my GED and I just haven't had a single second to study because I was hauling materials up the stairs all day. That kind of everyday stress is definitely there. But actual anxiety or depression? No, I really don't think so. \n\nI'm generally a pretty positive person, honestly. I like my life, I love my husband, I'm really proud of the work I do and being in the union. It's really just this one specific, incredibly frustrating thing with the elevators. It's like... if I just didn't have to deal with those stupid metal boxes, my life would actually be pretty great right now. It just makes me so mad because it's this one ridiculous fear that makes me sweat and panic and run late, but the second I'm away from it? Like, once I'm actually doing the framing work on the site, or when I'm just sitting on the couch at home with my husband watching TV, I feel completely normal. Totally fine. Just... really, really tired, like I said. But definitely not depressed.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing any feelings of anxiety or depression when you're not thinking about or using elevators?\n\nPatient: I think... wait, didn't you just ask me that? Or something really similar? But yeah, no, like I was just saying a second ago, definitely not. I mean, I really hope I'm explaining this right, but when I'm not actively staring at a set of elevator doors or dreading having to go up to my GED class, my brain is completely fine. I'm not sitting around worrying about random things or feeling down in the dumps at all. \n\nLike, just yesterday evening, my husband and I were trying to grill some chicken out on our little patio—it was super windy, so it took absolutely forever to get the charcoal going—but we were just hanging out, talking about his day, and I wasn't thinking about elevators at all. And I felt perfectly happy! Well, you know, my legs were aching from taking the stairs all week like I told you, but mentally? Totally fine. \n\nIt's literally just the elevators that make me feel that awful, panicky, out-of-control way. If I'm not looking at one or stressing about having to get inside one for work, I'm just regular old me. Just a normal thirty-four-year-old trying to get her diploma and do her job, you know? So, yeah, no other anxiety or depression or anything like that. Just the normal everyday stuff.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your fear of elevators is affecting your overall happiness or sense of fulfillment in life?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say it's affecting my overall happiness or fulfillment. I mean, like I said, I'm generally a pretty happy person. I love my husband, I'm proud of the work I do, and I'm really excited about getting my GED. I'm actually looking forward to the future, you know? Like, once I have that diploma, I can maybe look into some specialized training, maybe become a heavy equipment operator or something. That's a real goal of mine. \n\nSo, no, I don't feel unfulfilled or unhappy in the grand scheme of things. It's just... this one thing. It's like having a pebble in your shoe, you know? You're still walking, you're still getting where you need to go, but every step is just a little bit annoying and painful. That's what this elevator thing is like. It's a massive, exhausting, sweaty pebble in my shoe. \n\nBut it doesn't ruin the whole walk. Like, when I'm on the job site and I'm actually doing the framing, or when I'm pouring concrete, I feel great. I feel strong and capable and like I'm contributing to something real. And when I'm at home, sitting on the couch with my husband, watching some ridiculous reality show and eating popcorn, I'm perfectly content. I'm not sitting there thinking, \"Oh, my life is so empty and meaningless because I can't ride an elevator.\" \n\nIt's just that the *process* of getting to those good moments is so much harder than it needs to be. It's the logistics of it all that drain me. The planning, the excuses, the physical toll of the stairs... it's just a constant drain on my energy. But it doesn't touch my actual happiness, if that makes sense. It just makes me really, really tired. And frustrated. Definitely frustrated. But not unfulfilled. I know what I want out of life, and I'm going after it, even if I have to take the stairs to get there."
